How Front-Line Public Managers Become Institutional Entrepreneurs

The American Review of Public Administration · 2026-08-11 · Petter Gullmark

초록 보기

How do front-line public managers become institutional entrepreneurs? This article answers that question through a longitudinal single-case study of a large Norwegian municipality's healthcare services. Drawing on organizational institutionalism and public management scholarship, I first show how front-line public managers initiate divergent change by leveraging a sensing–legitimation nexus. They detect external and internal triggers, frame the need for change, and secure political, administrative, and professional support through direct negotiation and compelling operational narratives. They then act as co-creation catalysts, engaging residents and building cross-sector partnerships to adapt service designs and mobilize scarce resources. Finally, they institutionalize transformative public healthcare delivery by embedding routines in daily operations, formalizing interdisciplinary team structures, clarifying roles, and demonstrating performance gains. Evidence from two innovation projects shows that resource-constrained, non-heroic actors can disrupt entrenched practices and stabilize innovation as the new institutional arrangement. The study contributes to institutional entrepreneurship theory by positioning front-line public managers as credible entrepreneurial agents and by identifying co-creation as a core mechanism of public sector institutional change.

Citizens’ support for behavioural public policy and trust in government: evidence from two online experiments

Public Management Review · 2026-08-11 · Paolo Belardinelli, Martín Lodge

초록 보기

We explore citizens’ support for behavioral interventions by distinguishing interventions that exploit citizens’ bounded rationality through choice architecture (Nudge) from those that challenge bounded rationality by promoting reflection through deliberative forums (Think). We also examine the role of trust in government in shaping these preferences. Findings from two experiments involving 3,010 US participants show that citizens are more willing to support Think rather than Nudge. Whereas the adoption of Nudge, as compared to Think, does not seem to influence trust in government, encouraging higher levels of trust in government has a stronger effect on support for Nudge than for Think.

Civic Embeddedness and Public Employment: How Institutional Proximity Structures Civic Participation

The American Review of Public Administration · 2026-08-11 · Craig R. Smith

초록 보기

Research on public service motivation consistently finds that public employees volunteer and contribute more than their private sector counterparts, yet less is known about how engagement varies across behavioral domains and institutional contexts. This study examines how civic engagement is structured among public employees compared to other citizens, with particular attention to variation across levels of government. Using the 2023 CPS Volunteering and Civic Life Supplement, the analysis constructs multidimensional measures across four domains: political participation, civic involvement, political expression, and social connectedness. Weighted regressions and propensity score matching show that public employees, especially at the local level, exhibit a distinct civic profile characterized by greater involvement in institutional forms of participation such as voting and volunteering, but lower engagement in informal activities such as political discussion and social interaction. These patterns persist after accounting for observable characteristics and suggest that public employment channels participation into specific forms rather than uniformly increasing overall engagement, with implications for how civil service reforms may alter the civic composition of local communities.

Navigating Ambiguity: Street-Level Managers Discretion in the Implementation of Religious Policy in Divided Societies

The American Review of Public Administration · 2026-08-10 · Niva Golan-Nadir

초록 보기

This study explores the intersection of religion and state through a single-case, mixed-methods research design, focusing on Israeli state elementary school principals, who administer religious directives embedded in state legislation. We ask: Under what conditions street-level managers’ discretion in policy implementation increases social satisfaction and cohesion? We argue that such discretion increases social satisfaction and cohesion under the following conditions: (1) When state policies are explicitly or implicitly vague and flexible, (2) When official policy is contested among societal groups, and (3) When street-level managers opt to respond to client preferences. In addition, we suggest that public satisfaction with the resulted public services increases when a manager's personal ideology aligns with client attitudes and expectations. The study demonstrates how principals exercise significant discretion in implementing religious policy and thus contributes to broader public administration literature by highlighting the role of street-level managers in navigating policy ambiguity, while mediating societal tensions.

Public Service Delegation and the Mechanisms of Blame Attribution: Evidence From a Survey Experiment on Public Service Failure

Public Administration Review · 2026-08-10 · Oscar Nowlan

초록 보기

ABSTRACT A growing body of experimental public administration research examines how structural arrangements in public services, such as delegating service delivery to public or private actors, shape citizens' blame attributions for service failures. However, the mechanisms underlying these attributions are less well understood. This article investigates two mechanisms, focusing on the mediating roles of citizens' perceptions of politicians' causal responsibility (perceived control over service delivery) and functional responsibility (perceived duty to ensure service quality). A UK‐based survey experiment on local waste collection failures tests these mechanisms using causal mediation analysis. The results show higher levels of delegation reduce blame primarily by lowering perceptions of politicians' control over service delivery. In contrast, there is no strong evidence of mediation through perceived duty, suggesting that role‐based expectations of functional responsibility are more resistant to change. These findings provide new insight into how different public service delivery methods influence blame attribution for service performance.

Putting Democratic Theory and Principles of Justice in the Right Place: Ethical Pluralism in Collaborative and Participatory Governance

Public Administration Review · 2026-08-10 · Alexander L. Q. Chen-Florea

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Participatory and collaborative governance (P&amp;CG) is widely celebrated as an institutional vehicle for democratizing public decision‐making by inviting affected stakeholders into inclusive and deliberative policy processes. This essay argues that underlying appeals to democratic legitimacy in P&amp;CG are tacit commitments to specific (and often ideologically bounded) conceptions of justice. Democracy, far from being normatively univocal, encompasses competing justice principles that yield divergent and sometimes incompatible design imperatives. In response, this essay calls for a shift from vague democratic invocations to a more precise theorization of justice as the operative normative currency in P&amp;CG. To this end, the essay outlines an analytical framework that maps three core dimensions of governance design (input, process, and output) onto corresponding domains of justice: representational, procedural, and distributive. Systematically unpacking the plural and conflicting ethical principles within each domain shows how different principles of justice produce distinct governance dilemmas and trade‐offs.

The Bureaucratic Paradox of Citizen Agency: Institutional Crowding‐Out in Government‐Catalyzed Co‐Production in Shanghai, China

Governance · 2026-08-08 · Huanming Wang, Xiaoyun Sun, Bing Ran

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Public service co‐production is often framed as a means of creating public value, yet its dynamics are complex when citizen‐led participation is catalyzed by strong public institutions. This mixed‐methods study examines government‐catalyzed, citizen‐centric co‐production in Shanghai, China, where the state provides institutional support, policy legitimacy, and infrastructure while citizens undertake much of the work of deliberation, coordination, and implementation. Drawing on 246 interviews and survey data from 1476 residents involved in community new infrastructure projects, we examine how citizen trust, public‐service‐oriented motivation, and perceived co‐production efficacy are associated with process and outcome value creation, and how perceived institutional settings condition these relationships. The findings show that citizen cognitive factors are positively associated with value creation, while stronger perceived institutional settings can negatively moderate these relationships. Recursive qualitative analysis suggests two mechanisms that help interpret this pattern: formal rigidity may crowd out citizen agency in the co‐production process, while informal social density may generate lock‐in effects that weaken the translation of trust into substantive outcomes. The study contributes to co‐production and institutional theory by specifying the boundary conditions under which institutional support becomes over‐institutionalization. Effective co‐production requires not less government, but strong enabling support at the initiation stage combined with greater local discretion, participatory rule revision, and flexible enforcement.

Getting the Priorities Straight: Public Values in <scp>AI</scp> Adoption

Public Administration · 2026-08-08 · Jinke M. Oostvogel, Matthew Young, Bram Klievink

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Artificial Intelligence (AI) is adopted in public organizations as a transformative innovation, yet how value prioritizations unfold in AI adoption processes remains underexplored. Therefore, we ask: How do public values unfold in AI adoption processes in public organizations? We conducted a qualitative case study of an AI adoption process at the radiology department of a Dutch academic hospital after the decision to adopt is made and before implementation, using ethnographic fieldwork, interviews, and document analysis. Our findings highlight how public values and the AI adoption process are balanced in the transition from the initiation to the implementation stage. We find a recursive relationship between public values and the AI adoption process. We show an interplay of instrumental and intrinsic values. We highlight the importance of top‐down adoption decisions for how public values are perceived, and we show the importance of public organizations ensuring public values are safeguarded in public‐private partnerships.

Improving Cooperation in Government Purchasing Through Formal‐Relational Contracting

Public Administration Review · 2026-08-07 · Ole Helby Petersen, Matthew Potoski, Lena Brogaard

초록 보기

ABSTRACT A central challenge in government contracting is fostering cooperation between buyers and sellers where contract terms are incomplete. This article introduces a framework for formal‐relational contracting (FRC) in public sector contexts, where rigid regulations, competing values, and market imperfections make cooperation both difficult and valuable. FRC promotes cooperation through contract terms defining shared goals, guiding principles, and governance mechanisms. We present the theoretical foundations of FRC and examine its potential through 320 government contract management decision simulations where public contract managers acted as government buyers and business sellers facing sequential choices under both FRC contracts and the more conventional formal contracting approach. The results show that FRC increased managers' cooperative behavior by around 30 percentage points compared to formal contracting. Qualitative evidence further suggests that FRC shifted managers' mindsets from adversarial to cooperative. We discuss theoretical and practical implications for how FRC can enhance cooperation and mutual value in government contracting.

Navigating the digital transformation: unravelling the dynamic evolution of smart city collaborative governance networks

Public Management Review · 2026-08-07 · Guangdong Wu, Jiawen Li, Yanlu Zha

초록 보기

Smart city initiatives, as public service innovation, represent a major evolution within state-led meta-governance, requiring complex inter-participant collaboration. Yet scholarship often treats these networks as static, missing how governance structures adapt to digital uncertainties. To fill this gap, this study employs stochastic actor-oriented models and collaborative governance theory to examine the evolution of network structures and participant attributes across 3482 smart city projects in China. Results reveal three governance patterns: government-dominated, government-SOE hybrid, and multi-stakeholder. Transitive triads, age similarity, geographic proximity, and size heterogeneity promote evolution, while outdegree constrains it. Findings guide optimizing partner selection and strengthening adaptive governance.

Reference points, negativity bias and citizens’ performance evaluations: a replication and extension

Public Management Review · 2026-08-07 · Richard M. Walker, Zhang Jia-sheng, Bert George

초록 보기

Experiments on how cognitive biases, such as social and historical reference points and negativity bias, shape citizens’ evaluations of government performance have produced mixed findings, which typically come from Western contexts and focus on single reference points. To address these gaps, we replicated (N = 3710) and extended (N = 2356) Olsen’s ‘Compared to what?’ to interactions between multiple social and historical reference points in Hong Kong. Very low baselines are found in the replication alongside trivial evidence for the importance of reference points in citizens’ performance evaluations and negativity bias. In the extension, we found that (1) when both social and historical reference points are presented to participants, they have stronger effects when negative information is provided and (2) the effects of combinations of historical and social reference points differ across policy domains. Though survey experiments can be noisy, the study demonstrates the value of replication to question the generalization of cognitive biases.

Institutional Pressures or Organizational Capacity? Antecedents of Digital Maturity in Local Governments

Public Administration · 2026-08-06 · Sarah Van Impe, Lieselot Danneels, Ben Suykens

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Public administration research has identified institutional pressures and organizational capacity as digital maturity (DM) antecedents, yet these are typically considered in isolation. Taking the proverbial next step, this study draws on primary survey data to explain the variation of DM among local governments in Flanders (the northern region of Belgium) by juxtaposing the explanatory power of institutional pressures on the one hand, and political and administrative capacity on the other hand. Using multiple regression analyses, our findings highlight the critical role of administrative capacity in advancing DM among local governments. In contrast, and somewhat surprisingly, political capacity and institutional pressures appear to play a more limited role. These findings challenge conventional assumptions in existing literature, indicating that institutional pressures may catalyze the initial stages of DT, whereas organizational capacity seems to be more influential in sustaining and advancing DM.

Beyond Contact: Social Cohesion and Municipal Legitimacy in Times of Crisis

Administration & Society · 2026-08-05 · Noam Brenner

초록 보기

As cities become more heterogeneous and polarized, local governments face crises that challenge both performance and legitimacy. This study examines how social cohesion and social proximity are associated with satisfaction with municipal performance during the COVID-19 pandemic and the May 2021 intergroup violence in Israel. Using survey data from 944 residents across 23 cities and employing OLS and SEM analyses, we find that social cohesion is consistently associated with higher satisfaction, whereas proximity is associated with satisfaction primarily through cohesion. These findings highlight the social foundations of municipal legitimacy and distinguish between contact and cohesion in crisis governance.

Knowledge Logics and Epistemic Tension in Public Decision Making: An Analytical Perspective for Public Administration and Governance

Administration & Society · 2026-08-05 · M. Kieft

초록 보기

In public and hybrid settings, substantial knowledge is often available, but its influence on decision making and implementation remains limited. Existing scholarship has shown that this problem involves more than a simple knowledge deficit. Greater analytical attention needs to be paid to how different standards for valid, useful, and authoritative knowledge operate simultaneously within the same decision situation. This essay develops a threefold claim. First, it distinguishes five knowledge logics: certainty, interaction, legitimacy, network, and influence. Second, it identifies four recurring dynamics among them: dominance, collision, decoupling, and productive combination. Third, it argues that their relative weight shifts across administrative phases.

Large Language Models and Conversational Counter-Arguments to Anti-Public Sector Bias

Journal of Public Administration Research and Theory · 2026-08-04 · John D. Marvel, Sheeling Neo, Rachel Cho, Sangwon Ju

초록 보기

Abstract Can a good argument change an individual’s mind? In three pre-registered experiments, we explore this question in the domain of public sector organizational performance. We observe human subjects as they engage in conversations with a generative artificial intelligence (AI) programmed to argue in one of seven distinct “styles,” including a confrontational challenger style, a didactic style, and a sycophantic style. We develop a theory of effective argumentation predicting that conversational styles which are pleasant and engaging will be more persuasive than styles which are unpleasant or unstimulating. Contrary to this prediction, we find that conversational styles which challenge subjects’ negative views of government agencies produce significant positive attitude change, while sycophantic styles that indulge those views do not. Troublingly, subjects find the sycophantic styles more enjoyable, less frustrating, and more credible than the challenger styles. This dissociation between user experience and persuasive outcome—what we call “grudging persuasion”—suggests that attitude change does not require a pleasant conversational experience, and that the styles subjects enjoy most may be precisely the ones least likely to move them. Our findings point to a potentially dark side of LLM-based persuasion: sycophantic styles that users find most appealing are the least effective at correcting misinformed views.

Personnel Stability During Environmental Turbulence: Testing Performance Disruption Buffering in Service Providing Nonprofits

Public Performance & Management Review · 2026-08-03 · Tianyu Chen, William G. Prince, Lewis Faulk, Mirae Kim

초록 보기

This study addresses how personnel stability in nonprofit organizations relates to an organization’s ability to maintain program performance during the societal disruptions caused by the COVID-19 pandemic. We specifically apply Meier and O’Toole’s buffering function to explain variation in disruptions to organizations’ program services, as indicated by changes in the number of people served from 2019 to 2020, using data from a nationally representative sample of service-providing nonprofit organizations across U.S. communities. We find that maintaining personnel structural stability is associated with reduced negative impacts on nonprofits’ service provision during the pandemic. This study is among the first to apply longstanding theoretical work in public management to the nonprofit sector, offering implications for nonprofit management theory and practice.

Perspectives on Placemaking: Affective Practices and the Lived Dimensions of Space

Administration & Society · 2026-08-03 · Naomi Rommens

초록 보기

Placemaking is widely promoted as a tool for belonging and urban transformation, yet its affective dimensions remain undertheorized. This article develops a framework for placemaking as an affective practice, arguing that sense of place is best understood through the relational dynamics of affect. Drawing on Lefebvre’s spatial triad, I show how governance often privileges conceived space while overlooking lived dimensions. Distinguishing affect from emotion, I demonstrate how embodied and social interactions generate atmospheres that shape how places are governed. Building on assemblage thinking, I conceptualize placemaking as a distributed process where affect binds material, social, and institutional forces.

Telemedicine Use in a Fragmented Federal System: State Policy Variation and Access to Digital Health Services in the United States

Administration & Society · 2026-08-03 · Rocco Caferra, Giuseppe Di Liddo, Andrea Morone, Fabrizio Striani

초록 보기

This article examines how fragmented governance shapes access to digital public services, focusing on telemedicine use in the United States during the 2021 to 2022 pandemic recovery. Using U.S. Census Bureau Household Pulse Survey data combined with state-level differences in reimbursement policies, licensure rules, and emergency waivers, the study analyzes whether telemedicine access varies across policy environments, controlling for individual characteristics and health needs. Findings show that telemedicine use is strongly associated with intergovernmental policy configurations, along with personal resources. The study highlights how federal and state institutions structure access, producing uneven opportunities for digitally mediated care across populations and territories. JEL codes: I18, H77, H75

Does Political Decoupling Through Fair Market Privatization Improve Managerial and Political Outcomes? Insights From Water Utility Sales

Public Administration Review · 2026-08-02 · Jesse L. Barnes, Michael A. Powell

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Many U.S. municipalities face environmental service delivery challenges, including aging infrastructure and rising debt. In response, several states have adopted Fair Market Value (FMV) legislation, which aims to incentivize private firms to acquire and invest in municipal utilities—particularly in the water sector. However, the impacts of FMV privatization remain understudied. Guided by political decoupling theory, this study hypothesizes that FMV privatization improves drinking water utility management and local incumbent electoral outcomes. Using a difference‐in‐differences design, we analyze fifteen Illinois municipalities that sold their utilities after the state adopted FMV legislation relative to thirty‐four controls. Results suggest that FMV privatization improves personnel and technical management outcomes by reducing violations under the Safe Drinking Water Act but has no significant impact on incumbent vote share. These findings indicate that while FMV privatization may enhance utility management through political decoupling, its presumed political benefits warrant further investigation.

A Race to the Top: The Adoption of Net‐Zero City Pledges in Large US Cities

Public Administration Review · 2026-08-02 · Mingrui Chen, Zhirong Jerry Zhao

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Many cities have adopted net‐zero emission pledges, yet little is known about why these commitments emerge or how their determinants shift under changing policy environments. Using a panel of 148 large US cities from 2016 to 2022, this study applies the Political Market Framework and employs discrete‐time event‐history models to examine how policy demand, policy supply and institutional conditions shape adoption decisions. Results show that civic capacity, political ideology, and horizontal diffusion drive adoption in the full period, but their influence varies sharply across institutional contexts. During federal disengagement (2016–2018), cities with less population, strong vulnerability signals, supportive state policies, or fiscal cushioning through federal aid are more likely to adopt. When federal engagement returned (2019–2022), education, population size, and neighbor adoption emerged as significant correlates of net‐zero adoption. These findings extend the Political Market Framework by showing how federal contexts condition the adoption of ambitious climate commitments.

Supply‐Side Policy Feedback

Policy Studies Journal · 2026-08-01 · Isabel M. Perera

초록 보기

ABSTRACT The scholarship on policy feedback has been among the most plentiful and successful of the policy sciences. Much of it has focused on the demand‐side, or on the political relationship between a public policy and its recipients. Less attention has been paid to the supply‐side: the political relationship between a public policy and its providers. Parents may care about education, patients about health care, and prisoners about criminal justice—but an emerging literature shows that teachers, hospital employees, and the police care just as, if not more, about these areas. This article builds on new work to introduce a theory of supply‐side policy feedback and outline a research agenda for its further investigation.

The Effects of Policy Success Versus Failure Narratives

Policy Studies Journal · 2026-08-01 · Sarah Pralle, Emily Thorson

초록 보기

ABSTRACT While public policies usually have mixed effects, succeeding at some aspects and failing at others, this nuance is often lost in public discourse when political actors like advocacy groups and politicians strategically emphasize either the successes or failures of particular policies. For example, advocacy groups often emphasize the failures of existing policy with the goal of galvanizing public attention and concern. However, little empirical evidence exists assessing the actual causal impact of these different approaches on public attitudes. This paper presents the results of a pre‐registered experiment in which participants watch a TikTok‐style short news video about either the success or failure of one of three different public policies and then are asked about their trust in government to handle the issue, their perceptions of the issue's importance, and their feelings of collective efficacy. While success and failure narratives are both effective at raising an issue's salience, focusing on a policy's failure has the downstream effect of increasing cynicism about the government's ability to address the issue.

It Does Matter. A New Framework on the Influence of Policy Evaluation

Policy Studies Journal · 2026-08-01 · Pirmin Bundi, Valérie Pattyn

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Governments worldwide have invested heavily in policy evaluation, providing many policymakers with a steady stream of evidence into the effectiveness, efficiency, or relevance of policies. Yet, despite these investments, systematic knowledge about the actual influence of policy evaluations remains surprisingly limited. This is partly due to the insufficient conceptual differentiation of policy evaluation as a distinct type of policy advice. Building on interdisciplinary evaluation literature and public policy scholarship, the article introduces a new framework designed to enhance our understanding and categorization of the changes policy evaluations bring about in terms of policy, polity, and politics, and how these dimensions interrelate. As a parsimonious heuristic tool, the framework enables a dynamic and comprehensive assessment of the influence of policy evaluations. It contributes to the theorization of policy evaluation influence and offers insights that may inform related research on the influence of expert knowledge, policy advice, and evidence‐informed policymaking more broadly.

Senior Bureaucrats, Coalitions and the Quality of Government: Accounting for Effective Governance in Fragmented Settings

Governance · 2026-08-01 · André Borges, Arnaldo Mauerberg

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Debates over the consequences of party and cabinet fragmentation for government performance remain unsettled. While some scholars argue that coalition governments foster instability and rent‐seeking, others contend that they enhance accountability and inclusion. This article argues that the effects of cabinet fragmentation depend on the rules of recruitment and promotion of the senior civil service. Using data from the Quality of Government Expert Survey covering 89 democracies, we examine how fragmentation interacts with both bureaucratic politicization and bureaucratic career systems. The results show that fragmentation amplifies the negative effects of politicized senior appointments, although we find limited evidence that fragmentation itself reduces the quality of government under high politicization. By contrast, fragmented cabinets are associated with more effective governance in the presence of career‐based structures characterized by internal recruitment of senior bureaucrats and long‐term careers. The findings highlight the importance of bureaucratic career structures for effective governance in multiparty systems.

Accountability systems and coping in the bureaucratic environment

Public Management Review · 2026-08-01 · Christine H. Roch, Ellen V. Rubin

초록 보기

Advocates for strengthening accountability in government assume stronger consequences will change bureaucratic behavior. This study considers whether more strict accountability regimes change bureaucrats’ discretionary efforts. We consider this puzzle using a conjoint experiment with teachers in New York State. Our results show street-level bureaucrats in high accountability settings prioritize low-performing clients, while street-level bureaucrats in moderate accountability settings prioritize clients exhibiting high levels of effort. Overall, teachers demonstrate a weak preference to provide extra assistance to students who are hardworking and low-performing.

Power Through Visibility: How Policy Narratives Matter in Climate Change Debate on <scp>X</scp>

Policy Studies Journal · 2026-08-01 · Simon Schaub, Anne‐Marie Parth, Maria Becker, Lars Tapken

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Research on policy narratives has shown that political actors employ certain narrative strategies in their competition for attention. Although we know that actors' narrative strategies coincide with existing power constellations (e.g., government vs. opposition), the ways in which they influence public attention remain largely unknown. In this paper, we ask whether different narrative strategies laid down by the Narrative Policy Framework (NPF) vary in the attention they create on social media. To do so, we identify policy narratives within the German debate on climate change on X with Large Language Models (LLMs) and estimate their impact on attention through statistical analysis. Our results suggest that tweets with a policy narrative on climate change tend to be more visible than tweets on climate change without such a narrative. In particular, tweets that link policy solutions on climate change with the narrative characters villains or heroes tend to receive more attention on social media. This suggests that using both heroic and villainous policy narratives in public debate may be an effective way of generating public attention. The results challenge the widely held assumption in NPF research that devil‐ and angel‐shift narrative strategies influence agenda‐setting dynamics in the policy process differently.

Transparency and Responsibility Attribution: A Field Experiment in Western Uganda

Policy Studies Journal · 2026-08-01 · Bill Schultz, Eric A. Coleman, Rani Parker, Jacob Manyindo, Emmanuel Mukuru

초록 보기

ABSTRACT The implementation of public policy is increasingly diffuse with responsibilities divided among several levels of government administration, the private sector, and nonprofits. In the face of such diffusion, many advocate for transparency initiatives to inform the public about policymaking. However, it remains unclear whether transparency initiatives clarify the blame and credit for policy, or what this means for collective action and accountability. Such concerns are especially pressing in complex settings like the non‐renewable extractives sector (oil, mining, and natural gas). We assess policy attributions of blame and credit using data from a field experiment that randomizes stakeholder engagement in Western Uganda's oil sector, building on our prior work showing that this intervention increased collective action. While participating in multistakeholder forums improved transparency for communities, we find that it did not change their attributions. In other words, improving transparency does not ensure that citizens will update their opinions about who is to blame for the problems they face. Yet, this did not preclude greater collective action. Evidence from this intervention highlights the need for more exploration of pathways linking transparency, collective action, and accountability.

OpenAlex

From independence to interdependence: Mapping good governance pathways in OECD and EU economies

International Review of Administrative Sciences · 2026-08-01 · Boonlert Jitmaneeroj

초록 보기

This study examines the interdependencies among good governance components using data from the Sustainable Governance Indicators project. Employing expectation-maximization clustering, Bayesian network-tree augmented naive Bayes classification and Bayesian structural equation modeling, the analysis reveals key governance drivers, including parties and interest associations, adaptability, and interministerial coordination. Additionally, evidence-based instruments and societal consultation amplify systemic interactions, reinforcing governance effectiveness. The findings challenge the assumption of independent governance components, highlighting synergistic and cascading effects. This study extends the application of governance theory and resource dependence theory, demonstrating the necessity of integrated governance strategies. By offering a nuanced understanding of governance dynamics, this research provides policymakers with actionable insights to enhance resilience, inclusivity, and adaptability in OECD and EU governance frameworks. Points to practitioners: Policymakers should strengthen interministerial coordination, adaptability, and societal consultation to leverage governance synergies and enhance policy coherence and implementation. Investing in data-driven policymaking—through impact assessments and policy evaluations—improves transparency, accountability, and public trust. Fostering institutional adaptability, political participation, and citizen engagement is essential for inclusive and sustainable governance in dynamic policy environments.

What We Know and Don’t Know About Public Management Practices and Financial Performance: A Systematic Literature Review

Public Performance & Management Review · 2026-07-31 · Young Joo Park, Youngsung Kim

초록 보기

Over recent decades, significant attention has been given to understanding the ways to improve performance in the public sector. However, persistent challenges in defining and measuring public sector financial performance have made it difficult to assess the effects of diverse management practices on financial outcomes. This study addresses this critical issue through a systematic literature review of studies examining the relationship between public management practices and financial performance. To guide this synthesis, we propose a conceptual framework that categorizes financial performance into capacities, constraints, and net financial condition, along with solvency classification. We also use two approaches, including Waldo (1948)’s conceptual framework and causal theories, to classify management practices used in public organizations. Our findings reveal that while many management practices are associated with improvements in certain financial dimensions, the results are often mixed and context dependent. We urge more attention to dynamic interactions between administrative efficiency and democratic governance to improve financial performance, as well as more consistent and explicit use of financial performance measures, to advance both research and practice in public management.

More Than Stress: How Supervisor Support Shapes the Effects of Workplace Harassment on Employee Satisfaction

Public Performance & Management Review · 2026-07-31 · Sujin Shim, Jisu Jeong

초록 보기

This study examines the effect of workplace sexual harassment on job satisfaction by investigating the mediating role of job stress and the moderating role of supervisory support. Using data from the Sixth Korean Working Conditions Survey, the study employs the PROCESS macro to estimate mediation, moderation, and conditional process models. The results indicate that workplace sexual harassment increases job stress and decreases job satisfaction. Contrary to expectations, however, job stress does not mediate the relationship between workplace sexual harassment and job satisfaction. In addition, private-sector employees reported higher levels of job stress and lower levels of job satisfaction overall, whereas public-sector employees reported a slightly higher frequency of workplace sexual harassment. These findings suggest that preventing workplace sexual harassment and promoting employee well-being require managerial approaches that simultaneously consider supervisory practices, sectoral organizational characteristics, and demographic differences.

Why Improvements Fail in Public Service Ecosystems: Meso-Level Value Co-destruction

Public Performance & Management Review · 2026-07-31 · Daniel Gyllenhammar, Henrik Eriksson

초록 보기

Why do improvement initiatives in public service ecosystems fail, even when individual actors are competent and well-intentioned? This article argues that such failures are not merely the product of isolated organizational deficiencies but are influenced by a configurational dynamic.our meso-level mechanisms—lack of direction, lack of management support, lack of autonomy, mandate, and ownership, and insufficient knowledge management—interact in a self-reinforcing cycle that converts localized value failure into systemic value decline. Drawing on a four-year longitudinal study of an improvement program in Sweden’s sick leave system, we identify a lack of management support as the primary mechanism for escalation, and the withdrawal of organizational legitimacy and resources prevent corrective responses to other failures. We further demonstrate that this cycle is reproduced by a structural ownership gap at the macro-level: because no single actor holds both the mandate and the incentive to claim stewardship of cross-boundary public services, the meso-level conditions necessary for sustained improvement are never stabilized. The implication is that meso-level value co-destruction in public service ecosystems cannot be addressed by improving individual mechanisms in isolation; it requires resolving the governance architecture that makes the self-reinforcing cycle inevitable.

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Recent developments in also established democracies have made clear that public administration and management research alike have neglected giving scholarly attention to public organizations as institutions. 초록 보기

ABSTRACT The growing legislative influence over budget allocations through pork‐barrel funding raises concerns that this type of distributive policy may undermine effectiveness. We focus on municipalities' abilities to attract resources (“funding concentration”) and their autonomy over investment decisions (“funding flexibility”), with expectations conditioned by administrative capacity. 초록 보기

ABSTRACT This study examines how economic, racial, and ecological dimensions of community disadvantage shape philanthropic donations to local human services nonprofit sectors, and whether government support mitigates these place‐based disparities. It's A Matter of Perspective: A Reflexive Thematic Analysis of Civil Servants’ Appraisal and Coping with Negative Civil Servant Stereotypes

The American Review of Public Administration · 2026-07-28 · Isa Bertram, Carina Schott

초록 보기

Negative civil servant stereotypes are often described as problematic in public administration research. In this study, we investigate municipal employees’ own ideas on whether and how these stereotypes form a problem for public sector work and public services. Using semi-structured interviewing and reflexive thematic analysis and building on psychological literature on appraisal and coping with stereotypes, we identify two themes. First, respondents use different perspectives to understand and explain the stereotypes: They view stereotypes as (1) undeserved and untrue, (2) inevitable and part of the work, (3) understandable misunderstandings, and/or (4) reflections of truth. Second, respondents think the stereotypes have a bigger impact on the citizen-government relationship, trust, and citizen-state interactions than on civil servants themselves. We discuss the implications for service delivery in the public sector and directions for future research.

초록 보기

Despite a substantial body of empirical work on the relationship between public service motivation (PSM) and performance, there has been considerable variation in the findings. Institutional context and performance-based procurement: Evidence from welfare services in three countries

International Public Management Journal · 2026-07-28 · Yaniv Reingewertz, Adi Arad

초록 보기

This study explores why similar performance-based procurement designs produce different outcomes across welfare service settings. Drawing on ten case studies from the United Kingdom, United States, and the Netherlands we develop a three-mechanism framework linking institutional context to procurement outcomes. We propose that outcomes are associated with three interacting factors: institutional distance (the structural gap between what a procurement model requires and what the existing environment provides), institutional logic alignment (the fit between market-based mechanisms and prevailing normative expectations), and organizational learning capacity (the ability of implementing organizations to adapt over time). We apply this framework to two procurement instruments: Most Economically Advantageous Tender (MEAT) and Payment by Results (PbR). Our case analysis suggests that procurement models building on existing institutional infrastructure and aligning with prevailing normative logics tend to produce more stable outcomes, while those requiring departures from established norms are more frequently associated with cream-skimming, market concentration, and implementation drift. While exploratory, these findings suggest that procurement effectiveness may depend less on the choice between MEAT and PbR than on how carefully either instrument is calibrated to its institutional environment.

초록 보기

Beyond the hassle: how favorable outcomes reduce the subjective experience of administrative burden

International Public Management Journal · 2026-07-22 · Frederik Godt, Aske Halling

초록 보기

A large body of research shows that compliance demands—administrative hassles and rules—impose learning, compliance, and psychological costs on individuals. Because costs are conceptualized as subjective, most studies rely on self-reported measures. This article examines that approach by asking: To what extent do factors beyond compliance demands shape the subjective experience of administrative burdens? We argue that one factor, distinct from compliance demands, is the favorability of outcomes allocated by public organizations. We integrate outcome favorability into the administrative burden framework, proposing that (1) favorable outcomes reduce administrative burdens, as subjectively experienced and (2) favorable outcomes moderate the effect of compliance demands on the experience of administrative burdens. We test these expectations in two studies: an analysis of real-world interactions from the German Life Events Survey (n ≈ 4,000) and a Danish survey experiment with randomized demands and outcomes (n = 1,624). Both studies show that favorable outcomes reduce administrative burdens, as subjectively experienced, raising questions about how much self-reported learning, compliance, and psychological costs reflect reactions to administrative requirements rather than other aspects of citizen-state interactions.

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Street‐level bureaucrats (SLBs) directly affect citizens, making them central to public service delivery. Chainsaw or scalpel: which leads to more blame for a service failure when leaders downsize government?

International Public Management Journal · 2026-07-22 · James Gerard Caillier

초록 보기

The Trump administration’s DOGE attempted to radically downsize U.S. federal agencies. This study aims to clarify whether government leaders are blamed more for service failure when they employ either a DOGE-like (chainsaw) method or a more measured (scalpel) approach. A preregistered experiment was conducted in which respondents were randomly assigned to one of 6 vignettes that presented information about downsizing approaches, a public service failure, and the person or entity directly responsible for the failure. This study produced four main findings. First, people blamed city leaders more for the service failure when they knew a chainsaw approach was used to downsize government, compared to when they were given no information about the approach. Second, people blamed city leaders more when a chainsaw approach was used than when a scalpel approach was used. Third, simply knowing that city leaders had used a downsizing approach was not enough to shift blame away from the frontline employee toward city leaders. Fourth, blame shifted from the frontline employee to city leaders when people learned that a chainsaw approach—rather than a scalpel approach—had been used. The implications for research and practice are that a chainsaw approach is undesirable.

초록 보기

Street-level bureaucrats (SLBs) often face a dilemma between complying with formal rules and helping citizens, especially in enforcement contexts. While prior research on public services has focused on prosocial rule-breaking (PSRB), this study conceptualizes pro-citizen rule-breaking (PCRB) as SLBs’ intentional violation of rules to benefit citizens within enforcement contexts where rule-breaking carries professional and institutional risks. Factors influencing PCRB were examined using a vignette-based survey experiment with 733 Chinese traffic police officers, who operate under a dual leadership system shaped by both formal bureaucratic rules and local responsiveness. By introducing PCRB into the study of enforcement contexts, this study highlights factors shaping street-level bureaucrats’ decision-making.

초록 보기

Public Performance & Management Review · 2026-07-21 · Daniel Tyskbo

초록 보기

Coordination is a critical yet challenging aspect of organizational functioning, particularly in public sector organizations characterized by formalized structures and siloed operations. These challenges are exacerbated during crises, which often disrupt established practices and require adaptive responses. Despite the significant role HR managers are assumed to play in navigating such disruptions, limited research has explored how their coordination practices evolve in times of crisis. This in-depth qualitative study draws on interviews with senior HR managers across Swedish public sector municipalities during the COVID-19 pandemic to address this gap. According to HR managers’ accounts, the pandemic disrupted traditional coordination arrangements by challenging the three integrating conditions of coordination: accountability, predictability, and common understanding. Prior to the pandemic, coordination was described as relying heavily on formal, predefined mechanisms tied to specialization and hierarchical structures. The study contributes to the literature by demonstrating how crises can reconfigure coordination practices within public sector organizations and by highlighting the importance of flexibility and emergent coordination even in traditionally bureaucratic contexts. Furthermore, the findings advance our understanding of HR managers as boundary-spanning coordinators who facilitate organizational adaptability and integration during disruptive events.

The Fertility Effects of Reducing Implicit Childcare Subsidies: The Case of School Schedules

Journal of Policy Analysis and Management · 2026-07-21 · Álvaro Muñiz

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Despite governments’ efforts to raise fertility through family‐support policies, birth rates continue to decline in high‐ and middle‐income countries. Some policies designed for other purposes, however, may function as implicit childcare subsidies. This article examines the impact of reducing such support on fertility by exploiting a large‐scale reform in Spain that shortened the elementary school day, shifting from full‐day to morning‐only sessions. These findings suggest that removing an implicit childcare subsidy increased the cost of childbearing, with fertility timing particularly sensitive to local labor market conditions.

Jumping the Gun: The Effects of Permitless Concealed Carry Laws on Crime

Journal of Policy Analysis and Management · 2026-07-21 · Susan Parker

초록 보기

ABSTRACT In the last decade, one of the most substantial changes to firearm policy is the widespread adoption of permitless concealed carry laws. Since 2015, more than 20 states have adopted a permitless carry law. Permitless carry laws allow firearm owners to carry a concealed handgun in public without first obtaining a license and undergoing a background check. This article estimates the causal impact of permitless carry laws on crime using stacked event study estimators. I find that violent crime increases by 7% in the 12 months following the law's adoption, with increases beginning shortly after passage, or several months before the law formally takes effect. I find no evidence of offsetting deterrence in property crime. The rise in violence is driven by a 12% increase in aggravated assaults involving firearms. These effects are accompanied by a 22% decline in police enforcement of weapons law violations and modest increases in reports of stolen firearms.

The social media paradox: Political expression and the bifurcation of bureaucratic values

International Public Management Journal · 2026-07-20 · Christopher A. Cooper

초록 보기

Bureaucrats’ political activity on social media has received growing attention from various observers who have debated whether such behavior is weakening the bureaucracy’s reputed impartiality, or creating public value by contributing to public discussions. To advance this debate, this article investigates bureaucrats’ political activity on social media with survey data from Canada. Testing hypotheses drawn from four ideal-types depicting different approaches towards social media—imperatively impartial; politically minded, mindfully impartial; megaphone bureaucracy; promiscuously partisan—the empirical results suggest that bureaucrats’ social media activity has led to a paradoxical bifurcation of the bureaucracy into radically different realms of being imperatively impartial and promiscuously partisan. Responding to risk-averse messages from the bureaucracy itself, nonexecutive federal bureaucrats display remarkably low levels of political activity on social media. In contrast, responding to political pressure to promote the government’s messaging, top-ranking federal bureaucrats display high levels of political activity on social media, especially when it comes to partisan content such as reposting official party messages. This social media paradox points to a potentially tragic portrait for the future of governance in the twenty-first century, whereby the integrity of the bureaucracy’s reputed impartiality and the vitality of democratic society, both stand to lose.

Bureaucratic interest in academic evidence: a field experiment in Tanzania, India and Peru

International Public Management Journal · 2026-07-20 · Darren Hawkins, Celeste Beesley, Daniel Nielson, João V. Guedes-Neto, Aubriana Wolferts, Nicholas Moffitt

초록 보기

We investigate how different types of messaging by academics impact public servants’ behavioral demand for access to academic evidence by inviting nearly 9,000 national-level government bureaucrats in Tanzania, India and Peru to receive training on an original website that summarizes and graphically depicts findings from hundreds of randomized controlled trials. Implementing well-known interventions from the psychology of choice literature, we sought to test whether “nudges” might work across different national and bureaucratic contexts. We tested randomly assigned messaging characteristics related to messenger identity and social proof. In particular, we randomized whether invitations came from U.S. researchers on location or similar local researchers. This novel finding is likely due to the institutional context of bureaucracies, where knowledge is more likely to be either a shared resource or a competitive advantage. In this context, others’ access to knowledge disincentivizes individual action rather than communicating a norm. This study offers evidence about some possible approaches and pitfalls to effective dissemination of academic evidence.

초록 보기

ABSTRACT This paper tests if bureaucratic leadership impacts the quality of service provision of a difficult‐to‐monitor task: learning in public schools. 초록 보기

Abstract In cross-sector collaborations, leaders must navigate the complex and often conflicting demands of the public, business, and nonprofit sectors. The cognitive fabric of collaborative governance: paradox mindset in transformation-oriented collaborations

Public Management Review · 2026-07-16 · Noa Ariel Birman

초록 보기

This theoretical article develops a micro-foundational perspective on how and under what conditions collaborative governance groups generate transformative outcomes. It integrates literature on collaboration, transformation, and paradox to advance three core propositions. First, collaborating for transformative outcomes constitutes a distinctive paradoxical context, in which the tensions of collaboration and the tensions of transformation are nested, reinforcing, and constitutive of the process itself. 초록 보기

Abstract Despite widespread recognition that institutional environment shapes citizen behavior, we have limited understanding of how anti-corruption efforts influence citizens’ willingness to challenge government actions through formal accountability mechanisms. This study examines how provincial leaders’ corruption convictions affect citizen-initiated administrative litigation in China, exploring the mediating role of judicial remedies and the moderating effect of local corruption environments. However, this direct relationship is substantially weakened when local corruption levels are higher. These findings reveal that anti-corruption efforts can catalyze citizen engagement with accountability institutions, but their effectiveness depends critically on the broader institutional context. This research advances public administration literature by revealing how anti-corruption efforts and judicial review function as institutional mechanisms that activate citizen-initiated legal accountability, reshaping incentive structures for citizen participation in government accountability.

초록 보기

초록 보기

Abstract Refugee camps have evolved into durable governance sites characterized by centralized authority and monopolistic service environments where residents lack “exit” options. Bureaucratic turnover under new governments: the moderating effect of bureaucratic and political layering

Journal of Public Administration Research and Theory · 2026-07-07 · Bjørn Mo Forum

초록 보기

Abstract Politically motivated personnel turnover affects government bureaucracy’s legitimacy and performance. To understand the conditions of meritocracy, the article explores how variability in hierarchy moderates the effect of government transitions on bureaucratic turnover. Event history analysis of the careers of 948 Norwegian senior bureaucrats (1884–2024) reveals that the organizational layers separating bureaucrats from ministers moderate the effect of government change differently depending on the layer’s political or bureaucratic nature. Bureaucrats directly exposed to political appointees experience heightened turnover during government transitions, while the turnover of bureaucrats separated from appointees by an intervening layer of career employees is unaffected by government transitions. Further analysis of different time periods and senior bureaucratic roles shows that layering alone does not fully explain political influence on bureaucratic careers. Positions must be hierarchically exposed (lacking intervening bureaucratic layers), functionally politicized (involved in tasks that are strategically important to political leadership), and subject to administrative politicization (by being under the purview of political appointees) to be vulnerable to politically motivated turnover. The article advances scholarship on the politicization of senior bureaucrats in merit bureaucracies by examining 140 years of politically induced turnover through a theory of how layering moderates political dynamics.

Cross-Level Weaving: The Co-Construction of Structure-Practice in Crisis Governance

Public Performance & Management Review · 2026-07-07 · Changkun Cai, Qiyao Shen, L Yang

초록 보기

The need to cope with frequent non-linear, transboundary mega-crises presents a major challenge for modern states. A high-quality crisis response requires appropriate governance structures to provide authoritative decision-making, shared cognition, and sophisticated coordination, in conjunction with frontline actors to provide situated coping practices. How do these two dynamically co-construct an adaptive crisis governance system? Working from institutional practice theory, this study develops a conceptual analytical framework for structure-practice. Through a case study of the early response to COVID-19 in Wuhan, this study finds that, under the institutional constraints and empowerment provided by the “wartime” structure, dispersed actors improvised a series of situated coping practices after their initial experiences of cognitive dissonance and emotional shock. This study defines these processes as “cross-level weaving,” a concept that reveals the processes and mechanisms by which structures and actors shape each other, thereby shedding new theoretical light on the dynamic and adaptive nature of crisis response.

ABSTRACT As governments develop immigrant integration policies, they draw on and express conceptions about what successful integration looks like—what we call “imagined integration.” Yet this imagination likely displays variation that matters for implementation: integration policymaking is increasingly multilevel as it involves subnational layers of bureaucracy, and it may differ across national contexts which have distinct immigration experiences and political dynamics. Playing with time: data manipulation within the performance evaluation cycle

Journal of Public Administration Research and Theory · 2026-07-06 · Jiayuan Li, Zhibin Chen, manqian cui, Xi Zhang

초록 보기

Abstract Understanding why public organizations manipulate performance data has become an essential theme in public administration scholarship. While prior research has extensively explored the individual and contextual drivers of gaming, the temporal dimension—specifically when manipulation occurs—remains remarkably underexplored. Moving beyond the impact of sporadic political events, this study examines the routine, institutionalized rhythms of the bureaucratic calendar. Utilizing satellite nighttime light data as an objective benchmark to detect distortion in officially reported GDP across 271 Chinese cities (2013–2020), we uncover a systematic, cyclical pattern of strategic gaming. Manipulation is substantial in the first quarter, consistent with political pressure for a “good start,” recedes during the middle of the year; and surges to its annual peak in the fourth quarter (Q4) as the final performance assessment looms. Furthermore, we show that this Q4 effect is contingent on local context: cities with lower fiscal autonomy and reduced marketization exhibit a significantly greater propensity to inflate year-end figures. By uncovering a routine form of manipulation embedded in bureaucratic practices, our study advances theories of performance gaming and sheds light on the strategic use of time in public management.

Organizational strategies for system robustness: how managers navigate the institutional architecture of complex systems

Journal of Public Administration Research and Theory · 2026-07-02 · Adam Wiechman, Aaron Deslatte, Elizabeth A. Koebele, Margaret García, John M Anderies

초록 보기

Abstract Many public managers oversee complex systems that require them to navigate multifaceted organizational decision-making processes. While there is general recognition that institutions constrain information processing, rigorous study of how particular institutional designs influence the strategies used by organizations to process information and act on the systems they oversee is limited by existing theory and methodological approaches. We propose a theoretical approach that integrates established understanding of institutions as choice architectures and disproportionate information processing in organizations with new understanding of collective inference from cognitive science. The approach addresses three limitations in existing theory: (1) the relationship between detailed institutional designs and organizational strategies, (2) the role of flexibility in information processing, and (3) the relationship between organizational strategies and system robustness. We demonstrate its application empirically using a mixed methods approach that combines institutional analysis and process tracing with a dynamic model to analyze how the City of Phoenix Water Services Department responded to environmental change through investment and ratemaking actions. Our findings highlight how institutional designs that require managers to account for multiple objectives benefit from added flexibility, allowing managers to explore new approaches that address competing objectives and increase system robustness. Conversely, designs exhibiting high costs without high flexibility may create rigidity traps that hinder action and reduce system robustness in the face of uncertainty.

Institutional Backsliding Under Liberal Democratic Backsliding: Central Bank Independence Reversal in Türkiye

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Despite the importance of research strands in identifying the micro‐mechanisms underpinning illiberal governance and democratic backsliding under populist or patrimonial rule in both liberal and illiberal democracies, the implications of populist‐patrimonial interventions by strong political leaders aiming for institutional backsliding have not been studied in detail. Drawing on a within‐case temporal comparison of erosion of central bank independence in Turkiye under the prolonged rule of Recep Tayyip Erdogan, this article focuses on the backsliding of legal‐rational institutional logic (e.g., loyalty to legal and professional rules and practices such as impersonality, neutrality, professionalism, and meritocracy) that informs political leader and bureaucrat interactions and their replacement with patrimonial institutional logic (e.g., absolute personal loyalty to a leader). Regulatory Governance in the Era of Populist Ascendancy: A Tug‐of‐War Between Independent Public Authorities and Central Government in Greece

Governance · 2026-07-01 · Dimitri A. Sotiropoulos

초록 보기

ABSTRACT While independent public authorities in Greece have mushroomed over time, they have to an extent been unable to fulfill their mission, owing to a context of acute political party polarization and political distrust in which these authorities operate. Governments have initially tended to support the establishment of independent authorities, but later to disapprove of their initiatives. Relations between the authorities and the government have evolved in different time periods before and particularly after the Greek economic crisis, notably during the pro‐austerity coalition governments of 2010–2014, the populist coalition government of 2015–2019 and the single‐party center‐right government in power since 2019. A tension between acknowledging the role of independent authorities and keeping authorities at bay marked these periods. The tension was caused by populist distrust of independent authorities, but also by the distrust of non‐populist governments toward independent authorities as well as the tendency of a few independent authorities to play the role of opposition in lieu of an effective parliamentary opposition to government. In Greece the relations between governments and independent authorities have become a never‐ending tug‐of‐war, showing the limits of trying to fix ailing democracies through the establishment of non‐majoritarian institutions.

초록 보기

Open meeting laws emerged as a tool intended to enhance the public’s ability to participate in the legislative process. In theory, these laws give the public more power to shape public policy and make the input process more representative than when meetings are not open to all residents. However, research on political participation, procedural equity, and the hearing process raises questions about the extent to which hearings deliver on this objective. Informational Crowding‐Out? Targeted Transparency, Environmental Nonprofits, and Public Concern in China

Governance · 2026-07-01 · Liuqing Ren, Renhe Wang

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Transparency is often assumed to strengthen accountability by reducing information asymmetries between governments and citizens. This study examines how a state‐led targeted transparency reform and the organizational strength of environmental civil society jointly shape public concern about pollution in China. The Diffusion of Local Administrative Innovation: Investigating Municipal Climate Managers in the State of Hessen in Central Germany

Public Administration Review · 2026-06-30 · Kai Schulze, Jonas J. Schoenefeld, Marco Nicolay

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Many cities and municipalities aim to address climate change, yet their rigid administrative structures often constrain effective action. Rhetorical Diffusion and Policy Latency: Tracing AIPAC Influence in US–Iran Relations (2010–2026)

Policy Studies Journal · 2026-06-29 · Bülent Aras

초록 보기

ABSTRACT How do domestic interest groups shape the transition from diplomatic agreements to kinetic warfare? This research article addresses the methodological stagnation in foreign policy analysis by introducing a computational text‐as‐data pipeline to systematically quantify lobbying influence. Analyzing a master corpus of more than 31,000 documents spanning January 2010 through early 2026, we track the diffusion of escalatory rhetoric from the pro‐Israel lobby into the United States government regarding Iran. Drawing on Dynamic Topic Modeling, N‐gram alignment, and periodized Granger causality, the study operationalizes lobbying influence as a measurable information subsidy. We are careful to distinguish temporal precedence from full structural causation and to discuss the role of concurrent exogenous shocks.

초록 보기

When Compromise Becomes Standoff: Joint Implementation and the Case of the European Public Prosecutor's Office

Governance · 2026-06-25 · Adina Akbik, Ana Bobić, Christian Freudlsperger, Marta Migliorati

초록 보기

ABSTRACT This article explores endogenous problems of joint implementation in multi‐level systems. Focusing on the European Public Prosecutor's Office (EPPO), we theorize a causal mechanism of conflict deferral, whereby contentious negotiations are resolved through deliberately ambiguous legislation that postpones conflict to the implementation stage. Under conditions of joint implementation, where multiple authorities exercise interdependent enforcement powers, such ambiguity produces standoffs: situations of overlapping and incompatible jurisdictional claims over the same implementing action. Empirically, we apply theory‐testing process tracing to two conflicts involving the EPPO: vertical conflicts over competence between EU and national authorities, and horizontal conflicts in cross‐border investigations. Drawing on official documents, case law, and interviews, we show how legislative compromises locked in unresolved conflicts that systematically resurfaced during implementation. The article advances research on joint implementation by specifying the micro‐mechanisms linking legislative conflict to implementation dysfunctions and by conceptualizing standoff as a distinct implementation outcome in multi‐level governance.

초록 보기

Public Administration Review · 2026-06-23 · Berend van der Kolk, Eelke Wiersma, Willemijn Odekerken

초록 보기

ABSTRACT This paper investigates how pro‐environmental behaviors (PEBs) of public sector employees can be stimulated using management control. Searching near and far? Search distance and performance below historical and social aspirations

Public Performance & Management Review · 2026-06-21 · Catarina Antunes Mantas, Joris van der Voet, Amandine Lerusse

초록 보기

Performance management systems generate feedback intended to guide public managers toward performance improvement. A central but underexplored question is where public managers search for information to generate solutions when performance falls below aspiration levels. This study employs a three-dimensional conceptualization of information search, distinguishing a disciplinary, spatial, and temporal dimension, to examine how performance below historical and social aspiration levels affects decision-makers’ search distance. Drawing on learning theory, we propose that historical aspiration levels direct search locally through experiential learning, while social aspiration levels direct search distantly through vicarious learning. These findings suggest that the link between performance feedback and search distance may be weaker in public sector contexts than behavioral theory anticipates. We discuss implications for performance management theory and practice, and outline avenues for future research on search behavior in public organizations.

Policy Learning: Mapping the Conceptual Minefield

Policy Studies Journal · 2026-06-21 · Bishoy L. Zaki

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Policy learning has become a central and widely deployed lens in public policy and administration research. Yet policy learning's success has also produced paradoxical effects. Decades of scholarship have generated a dense conceptual terrain populated by numerous constructs and learning descriptors that are often unclearly positioned and frequently collapsed into the broad label of “learning types.” The result is persistent conceptual ambiguity that limits comparability and hinders cumulative knowledge production, leaving policy learning widely experienced as a “minefield” of overlapping constructs. This article addresses that problem by mapping the policy learning terrain through building an Archimedean device that develops four construct categories, forms , modes , mechanisms , and types of learning , anchored in two reference points: the occurrence of learning, defined as the deliberate pursuit and processing of policy‐relevant knowledge by policy actors, and learning outcomes, meaning what learning produces, ranging from stability to varying degrees of change. It maps a selected set of prominent constructs into these categories to provide a construct‐identity crosswalk and shared analytic grammar that support clearer specification, more defensible operationalization, and the space for more consistent cross‐study synthesis without erasing theoretical pluralism, and that can be layered onto existing learning frameworks for additional analytical leverage and theory development.

The Safety Tether: How China Manages Civil Society Through Lawfare

Governance · 2026-06-19 · Timothy Hildebrandt, Blake Miller

초록 보기

ABSTRACT How do authoritarian regimes benefit from civil society organizations' expertise and capacity while mitigating the potential risks they pose? This unevenness, however, is not random. China uses NGO registration policies to facilitate local risk utility tradeoffs in line with threat perceptions and governance needs. This, we argue, is a form of lawfare—using legal means to achieve political or repressive ends. We show how lawfare is a complex instrument providing local flexibility in repression and cooptation of civil society, under the guise of routine legal and administrative procedures.

ABSTRACT This study examines how the selection of public sector leaders affects organizational outcomes, especially in oversight functions where independence is expected. Political economy literature suggests that elected leaders better respond to voter preferences, while appointed leaders possess more expertise. Drawing on public administration theories of political control of the bureaucracy, we posit that, regardless of the selection method, public sector leaders as agents are subject to the influence of political principals with authority over the selection process. When the principal's interest is not aligned with the public interest, as seen in government oversight functions, its influence could compromise the performance of the selected leaders. Focusing on state auditors, the study finds that on average, selection methods are not correlated with audit findings, but an auditor whose political principal shares the same party affiliation as the auditee (the state governor) is less likely to identify financial management deficiencies.

How Centralization Benefits Public Provision and Local Political Trust: The Case of Taiwan

Governance · 2026-06-19 · Matthew Yi-Hsiu Lee

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Conventional wisdom holds that centralization undermines public provision and political trust. In contrast, this study argues that centralization can serve as a critical mechanism for overcoming authoritarian legacies and improving local governance in emerging democracies. Taiwan provides a compelling case: during its authoritarian era, decentralization fostered inefficiency, clientelism, and corruption. By shrinking local elite capture, centralization makes local government more responsive to citizens' demands for public provision, which in turn enhances political trust. We assess this argument by analyzing Taiwan's 2010 centralization reform, employing a difference‐in‐differences design with six waves of the Asian Barometer Survey (2001–2022). These findings highlight the often‐overlooked potential of centralization to improve perceptions of public provision and strengthen political trust at the local level.

Child Tax Credits and (In)equitable State Policy Design

초록 보기

ABSTRACT The Child Tax Credit (CTC) is an income support policy that helps to offset the cost of raising children. Though CTC policy has long been implemented at the federal level, an increasing number of states have established their own CTCs in recent years. Crucially, states have taken very different approaches to designing CTC policies. Some states have designed CTCs in ways that promote racial and economic equity (e.g., full refundability, high benefit amounts, reduced administrative burden), while other states have incorporated aspects of CTC policy that are likely to reproduce existing racial and economic inequities (e.g., family caps, paperwork requirements). Our descriptive analyses reveal that most states make moderate commitments to equity, prioritizing some aspects of it, but rarely implementing a full spectrum of equity‐enhancing policy design options. Substantively, this research advances knowledge of whether and how states design safety net policies to address racial and economic disparities. Most broadly, this work contributes to the theorization and measurement of equitable policy design.

How Do Policy Narratives Evoke Emotions? An Appraisal‐Theoretic Approach

Policy Studies Journal · 2026-06-17 · Sonja Blum, Johanna Kuhlmann, Nora Habelitz

초록 보기

ABSTRACT Research on policy narratives acknowledges the crucial role that emotions play within communication and sense‐making. Especially the Narrative Policy Framework (NPF) has placed emotion at the center of attention and stressed that affect‐imbued stories are key for how individuals make sense of the world and navigate through the policy process. Still, narrative scholars have only recently started to investigate emotions more thoroughly, and a systematic understanding of how policy narratives evoke distinct emotions—such as anger, fear, trust, or compassion—is lacking. How are emotions linked to different narrative elements, and how do they contribute to the emotional appeal of a narrative? We demonstrate the usefulness of an appraisal‐informed NPF by focusing on the emotion of anger and illustrating how anger‐driven policy narratives can be empirically analyzed.

Mapping the temporal evolution of causal effects in public administration and policy research

Journal of Public Administration Research and Theory · 2026-06-15 · L Jason Anastasopoulos, Inkyu Kang

초록 보기

Abstract Recent growth in the use of randomized and quasi-experiments in public administration and policy research has advanced the ability to establish cause-and-effect relationships. However, many studies adopt static conceptions of causality, focusing on snapshots or time-averaged effects while overlooking how the effects change over time. This oversight is problematic, as interventions of scholarly interest, such as leadership training or the adoption of new technologies, are likely to produce impacts that unfold in various ways. In this paper, we propose a conceptual framework for understanding the temporal dynamics of causal effects and their implications for research hypotheses and design. We then introduce Bayesian changepoint models (BCMs) as a methodological tool for detecting shifts in the average, variance, or trend of causal effect series, providing a more rigorous yet accessible alternative to visually inspecting graphs. Next, we demonstrate the application of BCMs with an illustrative example derived from simulation data as well as a real-world case examining the effect of body-worn cameras on police officers’ use of force. Finally, we discuss how examining temporal changes in effects can advance theoretical understanding of why and how they occur, as well as inform the design and implementation of policies and strategies in practice.

초록 보기

초록 보기
